========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I guess... my wife really pushed me to make the appointment. It's just that I don't feel real happiness or closeness anymore. Like, at all. Even when she tries to get me to enjoy our old Sunday routines. We used to do this whole Sunday morning thing with the paper and these specific bagels from downtown. The asiago cheese ones. Though honestly, you can't even get them anymore because that bakery got bought out by some corporate coffee chain. It's a real shame, they completely ruined the corner there, and their coffee always tastes burnt to me anyway. 

But... yeah, even when she tries to set up a nice morning like we used to, or, you know, if something at work goes really well... I'm a manager, and we just wrapped up this massive quarterly project that went perfectly, but I just... I didn't feel anything about it. No pride, no joy. Just flat. Speaking of work, I actually had to take a huge detour getting here from the office because they closed off Main Street for some water main repair, so I had to loop all the way around the highway. The traffic was unbelievable. But yeah, I just don't feel happy about things anymore.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's been... it really started right after the retrofit accident. That was about 1 year and 7 months ago. 

I remember the timing exactly because my nephew had just started his freshman year at Penn State, and my sister was calling me every single day crying about having an empty nest. She's always been a bit dramatic, honestly. Like when we were kids, if she got a tiny scrape on her knee, the whole neighborhood had to hear about it. My mom used to just give her a popsicle to quiet her down. You know those cheap cherry ones with the wooden sticks that always made your mouth taste a little bit like cardboard? I haven't had one of those in decades. 

But anyway, the retrofit... we were updating the main warehouse grid at work, supposed to be this massive state-of-the-art automated system. Cost the company absolute millions, and the contractors were a total nightmare to deal with from day one. Leaving tools around, ignoring the site rules. I told upper management they were cutting corners, but nobody ever listens to the middle manager until the budget is threatened. 

But yeah, ever since the accident happened during all that, a year and seven months ago... I've just been completely blank.

No, no, I've never really been a depressed kind of guy. Always pretty steady, you know? Even when things got stressful. Like when we bought our first house, the inspection missed this huge issue with the foundation. At home, my marriage feels strained because I'm present physically but not really participating in our life together.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 7 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since the retrofit accident about 1 year and 7 months ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 47 / manager / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S076\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... my wife really pushed me to make the appointment. It's just that I don't feel real happiness or closeness anymore. Like, at all. Even when she tries to get me to enjoy our old Sunday routines. We used to do this whole Sunday morning thing with the paper and these specific bagels from downtown. The asiago cheese ones. Though honestly, you can't even get them anymore because that bakery got bought out by some corporate coffee chain. It's a real shame, they completely ruined the corner there, and their coffee always tastes burnt to me anyway. \n\nBut... yeah, even when she tries to set up a nice morning like we used to, or, you know, if something at work goes really well... I'm a manager, and we just wrapped up this massive quarterly project that went perfectly, but I just... I didn't feel anything about it. No pride, no joy. Just flat. Speaking of work, I actually had to take a huge detour getting here from the office because they closed off Main Street for some water main repair, so I had to loop all the way around the highway. The traffic was unbelievable. But yeah, I just don't feel happy about things anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, without any real happiness or closeness?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been... it really started right after the retrofit accident. That was about 1 year and 7 months ago. \n\nI remember the timing exactly because my nephew had just started his freshman year at Penn State, and my sister was calling me every single day crying about having an empty nest. She's always been a bit dramatic, honestly. Like when we were kids, if she got a tiny scrape on her knee, the whole neighborhood had to hear about it. My mom used to just give her a popsicle to quiet her down. You know those cheap cherry ones with the wooden sticks that always made your mouth taste a little bit like cardboard? I haven't had one of those in decades. \n\nBut anyway, the retrofit... we were updating the main warehouse grid at work, supposed to be this massive state-of-the-art automated system. Cost the company absolute millions, and the contractors were a total nightmare to deal with from day one. Leaving tools around, ignoring the site rules. I told upper management they were cutting corners, but nobody ever listens to the middle manager until the budget is threatened. \n\nBut yeah, ever since the accident happened during all that, a year and seven months ago... I've just been completely blank.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt extremely down or depressed before the retrofit accident?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never really been a depressed kind of guy. Always pretty steady, you know? Even when things got stressful. Like when we bought our first house, the inspection missed this huge issue with the foundation. We had to dig up the entire front yard, right after my wife had planted these incredible hydrangeas she ordered from some specialty nursery in Oregon. Cost a fortune, those plants. And the shipping alone was ridiculous because they had to come in these climate-controlled boxes. I remember the delivery guy complaining about how heavy they were, and he backed his truck right into our mailbox. Completely bent the post. I had to spend an entire Saturday pouring new concrete just to fix it, and the concrete mix I bought from the hardware store was half-cured in the bag because they store it outside in the rain. \n\nBut anyway, no, I never got down or anything like this before. This whole... feeling nothing thing... it's completely new since the accident.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you still enjoy activities that you used to find pleasurable before the accident?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really. I mean, I used to love playing golf on the weekends. My buddy Mark and I would go out to this course over in the valley... Oak Creek, I think it's called? Or Oak Ridge. Actually, it might have been Pine something. They had this incredible clubhouse with these leather chairs that were so deep you could practically fall asleep in them. Mark actually did fall asleep in one once, right after we played eighteen holes in the pouring rain. He had this ridiculous umbrella hat on that he bought at a gag gift store, and it kept blowing inside out. He thought it was hilarious, but the starter at the course was so annoyed with us. The starter was this old guy, military type, always wore his pants pulled up way too high. You know the type, right? Probably ironed his socks. 

But no... I don't really care about golf anymore. Or anything else, really. I haven't even touched my clubs since the accident. They're just gathering dust in the garage right next to that snowblower I bought three years ago and have literally used twice because it never snows here anymore. Global warming, I guess. Or maybe just bad luck with my purchases.

Honestly, no. That's actually what brings me in, to be honest. I just don't feel any real happiness or closeness anymore. Not even a little bit. My wife tries, she really does. She'll try to get me to enjoy our old Sunday routines, you know? We used to always go to this diner on Route 9 for breakfast. The place with the neon sign that's always buzzing and flickering. The 'E' in 'Diner' has been burnt out since like 2015, so it just says 'Din r'. I told the owner, Gus, about it a hundred times, but he's so cheap. He drives this immaculate vintage Corvette, but he won't spend fifty bucks to fix a sign. Typical, right? Reminds me of my old boss at the logistics firm, who would expense these lavish steak dinners but wouldn't approve my request for a new office chair when the wheels fell off mine. I ended up having to prop it up with a stack of old phone books. Do they even make phone books anymore? I feel like I haven't seen one in years. 

But yeah... even if we do something like that, or if something at work goes perfectly well... I just feel nothing. Just completely empty.

trying to help are broken up and partly just gone.\n- key: S072\n  name: Negative_Beliefs\n  description: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.\n  manifestation: I keep thinking I can't trust my own judgment anymore, like if I miss one detail on a schedule or a machine setting, somebody else is going to get hurt because of me.\n- key: S075\n  name: Social_Detachment\n  description: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.\n  manifestation: At home I sit through family meals like I'm behind glass, hearing people talk and laugh but feeling like I don't really belong in the room with them.\n- key: S077\n  name: Irritability_Aggression\n  description: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.\n  manifestation: I snap at operators, supervisors, and my wife over small questions, and there have been times I've slammed a clipboard or barked at someone before I even knew I was angry.\n- key: S079\n  name: Hypervigilance\n  description: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.\n  manifestation: On the floor I'm constantly checking gauges, exits, people's hands, and where the emergency stops are, and even in meetings I pick a seat where I can see the door.\n- key: S080\n  name: Exaggerated_Startle_Response\n  description: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.\n  manifestation: If a forklift backs up, a door bangs, or a plant alarm chirps, my whole body jumps like something is exploding, and it can take me several minutes before my hands stop shaking.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I've had to hand off floor responsibilities I used to manage myself, and I lost my composure during a regional director interview badly enough that I walked out. At home, my marriage feels strained because I'm present physically but not really participating in our life together.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 7 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since the retrofit accident about 1 year and 7 months ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 47 / manager / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S076\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt extremely down or depressed before the retrofit accident?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never really been a depressed kind of guy. Always pretty steady, you know? Even when things got stressful. Like when we bought our first house, the inspection missed this huge issue with the foundation. We had to dig up the entire front yard, right after my wife had planted these incredible hydrangeas she ordered from some specialty nursery in Oregon. Cost a fortune, those plants. And the shipping alone was ridiculous because they had to come in these climate-controlled boxes. I remember the delivery guy complaining about how heavy they were, and he backed his truck right into our mailbox. Completely bent the post. I had to spend an entire Saturday pouring new concrete just to fix it, and the concrete mix I bought from the hardware store was half-cured in the bag because they store it outside in the rain. \n\nBut anyway, no, I never got down or anything like this before. This whole... feeling nothing thing... it's completely new since the accident.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy activities that you used to find pleasurable before the accident?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I used to love playing golf on the weekends. My buddy Mark and I would go out to this course over in the valley... Oak Creek, I think it's called? Or Oak Ridge. Actually, it might have been Pine something. They had this incredible clubhouse with these leather chairs that were so deep you could practically fall asleep in them. Mark actually did fall asleep in one once, right after we played eighteen holes in the pouring rain. He had this ridiculous umbrella hat on that he bought at a gag gift store, and it kept blowing inside out. He thought it was hilarious, but the starter at the course was so annoyed with us. The starter was this old guy, military type, always wore his pants pulled up way too high. You know the type, right? Probably ironed his socks. \n\nBut no... I don't really care about golf anymore. Or anything else, really. I haven't even touched my clubs since the accident. They're just gathering dust in the garage right next to that snowblower I bought three years ago and have literally used twice because it never snows here anymore. Global warming, I guess. Or maybe just bad luck with my purchases.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasurable or enjoyable, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no. That's actually what brings me in, to be honest. I just don't feel any real happiness or closeness anymore. Not even a little bit. My wife tries, she really does. She'll try to get me to enjoy our old Sunday routines, you know? We used to always go to this diner on Route 9 for breakfast. The place with the neon sign that's always buzzing and flickering. The 'E' in 'Diner' has been burnt out since like 2015, so it just says 'Din r'. I told the owner, Gus, about it a hundred times, but he's so cheap. He drives this immaculate vintage Corvette, but he won't spend fifty bucks to fix a sign. Typical, right? Reminds me of my old boss at the logistics firm, who would expense these lavish steak dinners but wouldn't approve my request for a new office chair when the wheels fell off mine. I ended up having to prop it up with a stack of old phone books. Do they even make phone books anymore? I feel like I haven't seen one in years. \n\nBut yeah... even if we do something like that, or if something at work goes perfectly well... I just feel nothing. Just completely empty.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel anxious or worried about things?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Anxious? No, not really. I mean, I used to worry about normal stuff, you know? Like at work. I manage a team of about fifteen people, and before all this, I'd stress over the quarterly reports or whether the new scheduling software was going to crash again. We switched to this cloud-based system last year, and it was a total disaster. The IT guy, Kevin—nice kid, but he always wore these aggressively loud Hawaiian shirts, even in December. Like, buddy, it's thirty degrees outside, we don't need to see neon pineapples. 

Anyway, Kevin promised the migration would be seamless, but it ended up deleting all of our PTO accruals. I had people coming into my office panicking because they thought they lost their vacation time. My assistant, Brenda, was practically in tears because she had this massive cruise to Alaska booked. She'd been talking about it for months. Apparently, you can see glaciers calving right from your balcony if you book the starboard side. Or maybe it's port? I can never remember the boat terms. Port is left, right? Because they both have four letters? My uncle used to own a pontoon boat, but that's hardly the same thing as a cruise ship. 

But... what were we talking about? Oh, worrying. Yeah, no, I don't really get anxious or worry about things now. Even when stuff like that goes wrong at the office these days, I just sort of stare at it. I don't have the energy to feel stressed about it.

Guilty? No, I don't think so. I mean, my wife sometimes says I should feel guilty about how much I spend on coffee. I go to this little drive-thru place near the office every morning. I told upper management they were cutting corners, but nobody ever listens to the middle manager until the budget is threatened. \n\nBut yeah, ever since the accident happened during all that, a year and seven months ago... I've just been completely blank.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt extremely down or depressed before the retrofit accident?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never really been a depressed kind of guy. Always pretty steady, you know? Even when things got stressful. Like when we bought our first house, the inspection missed this huge issue with the foundation. We had to dig up the entire front yard, right after my wife had planted these incredible hydrangeas she ordered from some specialty nursery in Oregon. Cost a fortune, those plants. And the shipping alone was ridiculous because they had to come in these climate-controlled boxes. I remember the delivery guy complaining about how heavy they were, and he backed his truck right into our mailbox. Completely bent the post. I had to spend an entire Saturday pouring new concrete just to fix it, and the concrete mix I bought from the hardware store was half-cured in the bag because they store it outside in the rain. \n\nBut anyway, no, I never got down or anything like this before. This whole... feeling nothing thing... it's completely new since the accident.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy activities that you used to find pleasurable before the accident?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I used to love playing golf on the weekends. My buddy Mark and I would go out to this course over in the valley... Oak Creek, I think it's called? Or Oak Ridge. Actually, it might have been Pine something. They had this incredible clubhouse with these leather chairs that were so deep you could practically fall asleep in them. Mark actually did fall asleep in one once, right after we played eighteen holes in the pouring rain. He had this ridiculous umbrella hat on that he bought at a gag gift store, and it kept blowing inside out. He thought it was hilarious, but the starter at the course was so annoyed with us. The starter was this old guy, military type, always wore his pants pulled up way too high. You know the type, right? Probably ironed his socks. \n\nBut no... I don't really care about golf anymore. Or anything else, really. I haven't even touched my clubs since the accident. They're just gathering dust in the garage right next to that snowblower I bought three years ago and have literally used twice because it never snows here anymore. Global warming, I guess. Or maybe just bad luck with my purchases.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasurable or enjoyable, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no. That's actually what brings me in, to be honest. I just don't feel any real happiness or closeness anymore. Not even a little bit. My wife tries, she really does. She'll try to get me to enjoy our old Sunday routines, you know? We used to always go to this diner on Route 9 for breakfast. The place with the neon sign that's always buzzing and flickering. The 'E' in 'Diner' has been burnt out since like 2015, so it just says 'Din r'. I told the owner, Gus, about it a hundred times, but he's so cheap. He drives this immaculate vintage Corvette, but he won't spend fifty bucks to fix a sign. Typical, right? Reminds me of my old boss at the logistics firm, who would expense these lavish steak dinners but wouldn't approve my request for a new office chair when the wheels fell off mine. I ended up having to prop it up with a stack of old phone books. Do they even make phone books anymore? I feel like I haven't seen one in years. \n\nBut yeah... even if we do something like that, or if something at work goes perfectly well... I just feel nothing. Just completely empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or worried about things?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, not really. I mean, I used to worry about normal stuff, you know? Like at work. I manage a team of about fifteen people, and before all this, I'd stress over the quarterly reports or whether the new scheduling software was going to crash again. We switched to this cloud-based system last year, and it was a total disaster. The IT guy, Kevin—nice kid, but he always wore these aggressively loud Hawaiian shirts, even in December. Like, buddy, it's thirty degrees outside, we don't need to see neon pineapples. \n\nAnyway, Kevin promised the migration would be seamless, but it ended up deleting all of our PTO accruals. I had people coming into my office panicking because they thought they lost their vacation time. My assistant, Brenda, was practically in tears because she had this massive cruise to Alaska booked. She'd been talking about it for months. Apparently, you can see glaciers calving right from your balcony if you book the starboard side. Or maybe it's port? I can never remember the boat terms. Port is left, right? Because they both have four letters? My uncle used to own a pontoon boat, but that's hardly the same thing as a cruise ship. \n\nBut... what were we talking about? Oh, worrying. Yeah, no, I don't really get anxious or worry about things now. Even when stuff like that goes wrong at the office these days, I just sort of stare at it. I don't have the energy to feel stressed about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I don't think so. I mean, my wife sometimes says I should feel guilty about how much I spend on coffee. I go to this little drive-thru place near the office every morning. It's one of those tiny sheds, you know? Barely big enough for the two baristas inside. They have this one girl working there, Sarah, who makes the absolute best flat white. She told me she's studying to be a marine biologist. She was explaining all about coral bleaching to me the other day while I was waiting for my drink. Did you know that the Great Barrier Reef has lost like half its coral since the 90s? It's crazy to think about. I watched this documentary about it once on Netflix... or maybe it was Hulu? I can never keep track of all the streaming services we pay for. I just feel... nothing, really.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Tom's a good guy, but he's got this dog, a golden retriever named Buster, who barks at absolutely everything. A leaf blows across the driveway? Buster loses his mind. The mail carrier comes? It's like the end of the world. The mail carrier, Dave, he's been doing our route for almost a decade. He always wears shorts, even when it's freezing out. I asked him about it once, and he said it's because his knees get too hot in pants. Isn't that weird? Hot knees. I didn't even know that was a thing. 

But, uh, what did you ask? Oh, right, harming myself. No. I just... I don't really have the motivation to do anything, let alone something like that. I just kind of exist right now.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Moving slowly? Um, no, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed anything like that. I mean, my car has been moving a bit slow lately, though. It's this 2015 Honda Accord, and I swear the transmission is starting to slip. I took it to this mechanic down on 4th Street—Mike's Auto, I think it's called. Mike is this big guy, always has grease on his hands, no matter what time of day you go in there. He told me it was just a sensor issue, but I don't know, it still feels jerky when I shift gears. It's funny, actually, my brother had a Honda back in the 90s, one of those old Civics with the pop-up headlights. Remember those? He loved that car. Drove it all the way to California and back one summer. He brought me back this snow globe from San Francisco, with the Golden Gate Bridge inside. I kept it on my desk for years until the water got all cloudy and weird looking. It's probably in a box in the attic now. We have so much junk up there. My wife keeps saying we need to have a garage sale, but who has the time to price all that stuff? People always want to haggle over fifty cents, anyway. It's just not worth the hassle. 

But, uh, no, I don't think I'm moving or talking any slower. I just... I don't really do much of either these days.

The traffic was unbelievable. But yeah, I just don't feel happy about things anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, without any real happiness or closeness?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been... it really started right after the retrofit accident. That was about 1 year and 7 months ago. \n\nI remember the timing exactly because my nephew had just started his freshman year at Penn State, and my sister was calling me every single day crying about having an empty nest. She's always been a bit dramatic, honestly. Like when we were kids, if she got a tiny scrape on her knee, the whole neighborhood had to hear about it. My mom used to just give her a popsicle to quiet her down. You know those cheap cherry ones with the wooden sticks that always made your mouth taste a little bit like cardboard? I haven't had one of those in decades. \n\nBut anyway, the retrofit... we were updating the main warehouse grid at work, supposed to be this massive state-of-the-art automated system. Cost the company absolute millions, and the contractors were a total nightmare to deal with from day one. Leaving tools around, ignoring the site rules. I told upper management they were cutting corners, but nobody ever listens to the middle manager until the budget is threatened. \n\nBut yeah, ever since the accident happened during all that, a year and seven months ago... I've just been completely blank.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt extremely down or depressed before the retrofit accident?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never really been a depressed kind of guy. Always pretty steady, you know? Even when things got stressful. Like when we bought our first house, the inspection missed this huge issue with the foundation. We had to dig up the entire front yard, right after my wife had planted these incredible hydrangeas she ordered from some specialty nursery in Oregon. Cost a fortune, those plants. And the shipping alone was ridiculous because they had to come in these climate-controlled boxes. I remember the delivery guy complaining about how heavy they were, and he backed his truck right into our mailbox. Completely bent the post. I mean, I used to love playing golf on the weekends. My buddy Mark and I would go out to this course over in the valley... Oak Creek, I think it's called? Or Oak Ridge. Actually, it might have been Pine something. They had this incredible clubhouse with these leather chairs that were so deep you could practically fall asleep in them. Mark actually did fall asleep in one once, right after we played eighteen holes in the pouring rain. He had this ridiculous umbrella hat on that he bought at a gag gift store, and it kept blowing inside out. He thought it was hilarious, but the starter at the course was so annoyed with us. The starter was this old guy, military type, always wore his pants pulled up way too high. You know the type, right? Probably ironed his socks. \n\nBut no... I don't really care about golf anymore. Or anything else, really. I haven't even touched my clubs since the accident. They're just gathering dust in the garage right next to that snowblower I bought three years ago and have literally used twice because it never snows here anymore. Global warming, I guess. Or maybe just bad luck with my purchases.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasurable or enjoyable, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no. That's actually what brings me in, to be honest. I just don't feel any real happiness or closeness anymore. Not even a little bit. My wife tries, she really does. She'll try to get me to enjoy our old Sunday routines, you know? We used to always go to this diner on Route 9 for breakfast. The place with the neon sign that's always buzzing and flickering. The 'E' in 'Diner' has been burnt out since like 2015, so it just says 'Din r'. I told the owner, Gus, about it a hundred times, but he's so cheap. He drives this immaculate vintage Corvette, but he won't spend fifty bucks to fix a sign. Typical, right? Reminds me of my old boss at the logistics firm, who would expense these lavish steak dinners but wouldn't approve my request for a new office chair when the wheels fell off mine. I ended up having to prop it up with a stack of old phone books. Do they even make phone books anymore? I feel like I haven't seen one in years. \n\nBut yeah... even if we do something like that, or if something at work goes perfectly well... I just feel nothing. Just completely empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or worried about things?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, not really. I mean, I used to worry about normal stuff, you know? Like at work. I manage a team of about fifteen people, and before all this, I'd stress over the quarterly reports or whether the new scheduling software was going to crash again. We switched to this cloud-based system last year, and it was a total disaster. The IT guy, Kevin—nice kid, but he always wore these aggressively loud Hawaiian shirts, even in December. Like, buddy, it's thirty degrees outside, we don't need to see neon pineapples. \n\nAnyway, Kevin promised the migration would be seamless, but it ended up deleting all of our PTO accruals. I had people coming into my office panicking because they thought they lost their vacation time. My assistant, Brenda, was practically in tears because she had this massive cruise to Alaska booked. She'd been talking about it for months. Apparently, you can see glaciers calving right from your balcony if you book the starboard side. Or maybe it's port? I can never remember the boat terms. Port is left, right? Because they both have four letters? My uncle used to own a pontoon boat, but that's hardly the same thing as a cruise ship. \n\nBut... what were we talking about? Oh, worrying. Yeah, no, I don't really get anxious or worry about things now. Even when stuff like that goes wrong at the office these days, I just sort of stare at it. I don't have the energy to feel stressed about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I don't think so. Activities I used to enjoy? Yeah, I guess... it's more like I just don't feel anything when I do them. Like, I don't feel real happiness or closeness anymore, you know? My wife, she tries so hard to get me to enjoy things again. We used to have this Sunday routine where we'd go to the farmers market downtown. They have this guy who sells the most incredible sourdough bread. He bakes it in a wood-fired oven he built himself on the back of a flatbed trailer. It's fascinating, really. He was telling me once about the hydration levels in the dough and how the humidity affects the rise. I tried making sourdough during the pandemic, like everyone else on the planet, I suppose. I got this starter going in a mason jar, named it 'Doughy Tribbiani'—stupid, I know. But it just ended up smelling like old gym socks and I had to throw it out. The jar is still sitting under the sink because I can't bring myself to scrub the dried crust off the sides. We have this really abrasive sponge, but it scratches the glass. I bought it at that hardware store on Main Street, the one with the creaky wooden floors. They have a resident cat there, a big orange tabby named Rusty who just sleeps on the bags of potting soil all day. I'm actually allergic to cats, which is a shame because they seem so low-maintenance compared to dogs. 

But yeah... even when my wife tries to do our old Sunday routines, or even if something at work goes well, I just... I don't feel it. There's just no joy there anymore.

That was about 1 year and 7 months ago. \n\nI remember the timing exactly because my nephew had just started his freshman year at Penn State, and my sister was calling me every single day crying about having an empty nest. She's always been a bit dramatic, honestly. Like when we were kids, if she got a tiny scrape on her knee, the whole neighborhood had to hear about it. I told upper management they were cutting corners, but nobody ever listens to the middle manager until the budget is threatened. \n\nBut yeah, ever since the accident happened during all that, a year and seven months ago... I've just been completely blank.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt extremely down or depressed before the retrofit accident?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never really been a depressed kind of guy. We had to dig up the entire front yard, right after my wife had planted these incredible hydrangeas she ordered from some specialty nursery in Oregon. Cost a fortune, those plants. And the shipping alone was ridiculous because they had to come in these climate-controlled boxes. I remember the delivery guy complaining about how heavy they were, and he backed his truck right into our mailbox. Completely bent the post. I had to spend an entire Saturday pouring new concrete just to fix it, and the concrete mix I bought from the hardware store was half-cured in the bag because they store it outside in the rain. \n\nBut anyway, no, I never got down or anything like this before. Actually, it might have been Pine something. They had this incredible clubhouse with these leather chairs that were so deep you could practically fall asleep in them. Mark actually did fall asleep in one once, right after we played eighteen holes in the pouring rain. He had this ridiculous umbrella hat on that he bought at a gag gift store, and it kept blowing inside out. He thought it was hilarious, but the starter at the course was so annoyed with us. The starter was this old guy, military type, always wore his pants pulled up way too high. You know the type, right? Probably ironed his socks. \n\nBut no... I don't really care about golf anymore. Or anything else, really. I haven't even touched my clubs since the accident. They're just gathering dust in the garage right next to that snowblower I bought three years ago and have literally used twice because it never snows here anymore. Global warming, I guess. She'll try to get me to enjoy our old Sunday routines, you know? We used to always go to this diner on Route 9 for breakfast. The place with the neon sign that's always buzzing and flickering. The 'E' in 'Diner' has been burnt out since like 2015, so it just says 'Din r'. I told the owner, Gus, about it a hundred times, but he's so cheap. He drives this immaculate vintage Corvette, but he won't spend fifty bucks to fix a sign. Typical, right? Reminds me of my old boss at the logistics firm, who would expense these lavish steak dinners but wouldn't approve my request for a new office chair when the wheels fell off mine. I ended up having to prop it up with a stack of old phone books. Do they even make phone books anymore? I feel like I haven't seen one in years. \n\nBut yeah... even if we do something like that, or if something at work goes perfectly well... I just feel nothing. Just completely empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or worried about things?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, not really. I mean, I used to worry about normal stuff, you know? Like at work. I manage a team of about fifteen people, and before all this, I'd stress over the quarterly reports or whether the new scheduling software was going to crash again. We switched to this cloud-based system last year, and it was a total disaster. The IT guy, Kevin—nice kid, but he always wore these aggressively loud Hawaiian shirts, even in December. Like, buddy, it's thirty degrees outside, we don't need to see neon pineapples. \n\nAnyway, Kevin promised the migration would be seamless, but it ended up deleting all of our PTO accruals. I had people coming into my office panicking because they thought they lost their vacation time. My assistant, Brenda, was practically in tears because she had this massive cruise to Alaska booked. She'd been talking about it for months. Apparently, you can see glaciers calving right from your balcony if you book the starboard side. Or maybe it's port? I can never remember the boat terms. Port is left, right? Because they both have four letters? My uncle used to own a pontoon boat, but that's hardly the same thing as a cruise ship. \n\nBut... what were we talking about? Oh, worrying. Yeah, no, I don't really get anxious or worry about things now. Even when stuff like that goes wrong at the office these days, I just sort of stare at it. I don't have the energy to feel stressed about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I don't think so. I mean, my wife sometimes says I should feel guilty about how much I spend on coffee. I go to this little drive-thru place near the office every morning. It's one of those tiny sheds, you know? Barely big enough for the two baristas inside. They have this one girl working there, Sarah, who makes the absolute best flat white. She told me she's studying to be a marine biologist. She was explaining all about coral bleaching to me the other day while I was waiting for my drink. Did you know that the Great Barrier Reef has lost like half its coral since the 90s? It's crazy to think about. I watched this documentary about it once on Netflix... or maybe it was Hulu? I can never keep track of all the streaming services we pay for. My wife keeps saying we should cancel a few, but then they come out with some new show you just have to watch. \n\nBut no, I don't really feel guilty about things. I just feel... nothing, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Weight? Uh... no, I haven't really noticed any changes. My pants still fit the same, I guess. I buy all my slacks from this one outlet store over in Westlake. They have these really great sales around Labor Day, you know? Buy one get one free, or sometimes it's like forty percent off the whole store. The guy who works there, Jerry, he's been there forever. He always tries to upsell me on these silk ties. I don't even wear ties! Our office went business casual back in 2018. Best decision they ever made, honestly. I remember before that, trying to keep those collars crisp... it was a nightmare. My dry cleaning bill was through the roof. We used to go to this place called Sparkle Cleaners, but then they ruined my wife's favorite silk blouse. Just completely melted the fabric on the sleeve. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Less energy? Um, I don't know if it's less energy, exactly. I just haven't really noticed a difference. I mean, my electric bill was definitely lower this past month, which is nice. We finally got around to replacing those old single-pane windows in the living room. The draft in there during the winter was unbelievable. You could literally see the curtains moving when the wind blew. We hired this contractor, Steve, who drove this massive Ford F-250. He had to park it halfway on the lawn just to get it in the driveway, and it left these huge tire ruts that I still need to fill with topsoil. I went to the garden center last weekend to look for some, but they were completely out of the kind I usually get. They only had this organic compost mix that smelled like cow manure. The guy working there, a teenager with braces who looked like he'd rather be anywhere else, told me it was better for the grass anyway. I remember when I was a teenager, I worked at a grocery store bagging groceries. Worst job I ever had. People get so particular about how you pack their bags. Don't put the eggs on the bottom, don't crush the bread... like, obviously I'm not going to put a watermelon on top of your tomatoes. 

But, energy? No, I don't think my energy levels have changed. I just don't have the drive to use the energy I have, I guess.

I've just been completely blank.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt extremely down or depressed before the retrofit accident?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never really been a depressed kind of guy. Always pretty steady, you know? Even when things got stressful. Like when we bought our first house, the inspection missed this huge issue with the foundation. We had to dig up the entire front yard, right after my wife had planted these incredible hydrangeas she ordered from some specialty nursery in Oregon. Cost a fortune, those plants. And the shipping alone was ridiculous because they had to come in these climate-controlled boxes. I remember the delivery guy complaining about how heavy they were, and he backed his truck right into our mailbox. Completely bent the post. I had to spend an entire Saturday pouring new concrete just to fix it, and the concrete mix I bought from the hardware store was half-cured in the bag because they store it outside in the rain. \n\nBut anyway, no, I never got down or anything like this before. This whole... feeling nothing thing... it's completely new since the accident.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy activities that you used to find pleasurable before the accident?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I used to love playing golf on the weekends. My buddy Mark and I would go out to this course over in the valley... Oak Creek, I think it's called? Or Oak Ridge. Actually, it might have been Pine something. They had this incredible clubhouse with these leather chairs that were so deep you could practically fall asleep in them. Mark actually did fall asleep in one once, right after we played eighteen holes in the pouring rain. He had this ridiculous umbrella hat on that he bought at a gag gift store, and it kept blowing inside out. He thought it was hilarious, but the starter at the course was so annoyed with us. The starter was this old guy, military type, always wore his pants pulled up way too high. You know the type, right? Probably ironed his socks. \n\nBut no... I don't really care about golf anymore. Or anything else, really. I haven't even touched my clubs since the accident. They're just gathering dust in the garage right next to that snowblower I bought three years ago and have literally used twice because it never snows here anymore. Global warming, I guess. Or maybe just bad luck with my purchases.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasurable or enjoyable, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no. That's actually what brings me in, to be honest. I just don't feel any real happiness or closeness anymore. Not even a little bit. My wife tries, she really does. She'll try to get me to enjoy our old Sunday routines, you know? We used to always go to this diner on Route 9 for breakfast. The place with the neon sign that's always buzzing and flickering. The 'E' in 'Diner' has been burnt out since like 2015, so it just says 'Din r'. I told the owner, Gus, about it a hundred times, but he's so cheap. He drives this immaculate vintage Corvette, but he won't spend fifty bucks to fix a sign. Typical, right? Reminds me of my old boss at the logistics firm, who would expense these lavish steak dinners but wouldn't approve my request for a new office chair when the wheels fell off mine. I ended up having to prop it up with a stack of old phone books. Do they even make phone books anymore? I feel like I haven't seen one in years. \n\nBut yeah... even if we do something like that, or if something at work goes perfectly well... I just feel nothing. Just completely empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or worried about things?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, not really. I mean, I used to worry about normal stuff, you know? Like at work. I manage a team of about fifteen people, and before all this, I'd stress over the quarterly reports or whether the new scheduling software was going to crash again. We switched to this cloud-based system last year, and it was a total disaster. The IT guy, Kevin—nice kid, but he always wore these aggressively loud Hawaiian shirts, even in December. Like, buddy, it's thirty degrees outside, we don't need to see neon pineapples. \n\nAnyway, Kevin promised the migration would be seamless, but it ended up deleting all of our PTO accruals. I had people coming into my office panicking because they thought they lost their vacation time. My assistant, Brenda, was practically in tears because she had this massive cruise to Alaska booked. She'd been talking about it for months. Apparently, you can see glaciers calving right from your balcony if you book the starboard side. Or maybe it's port? I can never remember the boat terms. Port is left, right? Because they both have four letters? My uncle used to own a pontoon boat, but that's hardly the same thing as a cruise ship. \n\nBut... what were we talking about? Oh, worrying. Yeah, no, I don't really get anxious or worry about things now. Even when stuff like that goes wrong at the office these days, I just sort of stare at it. I don't have the energy to feel stressed about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I don't think so. I mean, my wife sometimes says I should feel guilty about how much I spend on coffee. I go to this little drive-thru place near the office every morning. It's one of those tiny sheds, you know? Barely big enough for the two baristas inside. They have this one girl working there, Sarah, who makes the absolute best flat white. She told me she's studying to be a marine biologist. She was explaining all about coral bleaching to me the other day while I was waiting for my drink. Did you know that the Great Barrier Reef has lost like half its coral since the 90s? It's crazy to think about. I watched this documentary about it once on Netflix... or maybe it was Hulu? I can never keep track of all the streaming services we pay for. My wife keeps saying we should cancel a few, but then they come out with some new show you just have to watch. \n\nBut no, I don't really feel guilty about things. I just feel... nothing, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Weight? Uh... no, I haven't really noticed any changes. My pants still fit the same, I guess. I buy all my slacks from this one outlet store over in Westlake. They have these really great sales around Labor Day, you know? Buy one get one free, or sometimes it's like forty percent off the whole store. The guy who works there, Jerry, he's been there forever. He always tries to upsell me on these silk ties. I don't even wear ties! Our office went business casual back in 2018. Best decision they ever made, honestly. I remember before that, trying to keep those collars crisp... it was a nightmare. My dry cleaning bill was through the roof. We used to go to this place called Sparkle Cleaners, but then they ruined my wife's favorite silk blouse. Just completely melted the fabric on the sleeve. The owner tried to blame it on the manufacturer, said the care tag was wrong. Can you believe that? The tag was perfectly clear. \n\nBut anyway... weight. No, I don't think it's changed. I just eat what I normally eat, I guess. I don't really pay attention to it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Harming myself? No, no, I haven't thought about anything like that. I mean, I don't really think about much of anything lately. I used to think a lot about fixing up the house, you know? We have this deck out back that I've been meaning to restain for, gosh, probably three years now. The wood is getting all gray and splintery. My neighbor, Tom, he restained his last summer. Used this redwood color that I thought was going to look terrible, but it actually turned out pretty nice. Tom's a good guy, but he's got this dog, a golden retriever named Buster, who barks at absolutely everything. A leaf blows across the driveway? Buster loses his mind. The mail carrier comes? It's like the end of the world. The mail carrier, Dave, he's been doing our route for almost a decade. He always wears shorts, even when it's freezing out. I asked him about it once, and he said it's because his knees get too hot in pants. Isn't that weird? Hot knees. I didn't even know that was a thing. \n\nBut, uh, what did you ask? Oh, right, harming myself. No. I just... I don't really have the motivation to do anything, let alone something like that. I just kind of exist right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Um, no, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed anything like that. I mean, my car has been moving a bit slow lately, though. It's this 2015 Honda Accord, and I swear the transmission is starting to slip. I took it to this mechanic down on 4th Street—Mike's Auto, I think it's called. Mike is this big guy, always has grease on his hands, no matter what time of day you go in there. He told me it was just a sensor issue, but I don't know, it still feels jerky when I shift gears. It's funny, actually, my brother had a Honda back in the 90s, one of those old Civics with the pop-up headlights. Remember those? He loved that car. Drove it all the way to California and back one summer. He brought me back this snow globe from San Francisco, with the Golden Gate Bridge inside. I kept it on my desk for years until the water got all cloudy and weird looking. It's probably in a box in the attic now. We have so much junk up there. My wife keeps saying we need to have a garage sale, but who has the time to price all that stuff? People always want to haggle over fifty cents, anyway. It's just not worth the hassle. \n\nBut, uh, no, I don't think I'm moving or talking any slower. I just... I don't really do much of either these days.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now you can't seem to find interest in them anymore?\n\nPatient: Activities I used to enjoy? Yeah, I guess... it's more like I just don't feel anything when I do them. Like, I don't feel real happiness or closeness anymore, you know? My wife, she tries so hard to get me to enjoy things again. We used to have this Sunday routine where we'd go to the farmers market downtown. They have this guy who sells the most incredible sourdough bread. He bakes it in a wood-fired oven he built himself on the back of a flatbed trailer. It's fascinating, really. He was telling me once about the hydration levels in the dough and how the humidity affects the rise. I tried making sourdough during the pandemic, like everyone else on the planet, I suppose. I got this starter going in a mason jar, named it 'Doughy Tribbiani'—stupid, I know. But it just ended up smelling like old gym socks and I had to throw it out. The jar is still sitting under the sink because I can't bring myself to scrub the dried crust off the sides. We have this really abrasive sponge, but it scratches the glass. I bought it at that hardware store on Main Street, the one with the creaky wooden floors. They have a resident cat there, a big orange tabby named Rusty who just sleeps on the bags of potting soil all day. I'm actually allergic to cats, which is a shame because they seem so low-maintenance compared to dogs. \n\nBut yeah... even when my wife tries to do our old Sunday routines, or even if something at work goes well, I just... I don't feel it. There's just no joy there anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have less energy than you used to?\n\nPatient: Less energy? Um, I don't know if it's less energy, exactly. I just haven't really noticed a difference. I mean, my electric bill was definitely lower this past month, which is nice. We finally got around to replacing those old single-pane windows in the living room. The draft in there during the winter was unbelievable. You could literally see the curtains moving when the wind blew. We hired this contractor, Steve, who drove this massive Ford F-250. He had to park it halfway on the lawn just to get it in the driveway, and it left these huge tire ruts that I still need to fill with topsoil. I went to the garden center last weekend to look for some, but they were completely out of the kind I usually get. They only had this organic compost mix that smelled like cow manure. The guy working there, a teenager with braces who looked like he'd rather be anywhere else, told me it was better for the grass anyway. I remember when I was a teenager, I worked at a grocery store bagging groceries. Worst job I ever had. People get so particular about how you pack their bags. Don't put the eggs on the bottom, don't crush the bread... like, obviously I'm not going to put a watermelon on top of your tomatoes. \n\nBut, energy? No, I don't think my energy levels have changed. I just don't have the drive to use the energy I have, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Concentrating? Making decisions? No, I don't think so. My focus seems pretty normal, really. I mean, I have to make decisions all day at work, being a manager and all. Like just yesterday, I had to decide which vendor to use for our new office chairs. The old ones were just terrible for your lumbar support. I had this one chair a few years ago that squeaked every single time I leaned back. Drove me absolutely crazy. I tried spraying WD-40 on the metal joints underneath, but it just made my whole office smell like a garage. My dad used to use WD-40 for everything, actually. He even sprayed it on his fishing lures because he swore the smell attracted bass. We used to go fishing out at this lake every summer when I was growing up. He had this little aluminum boat with a sputtering outboard motor that you had to pull-start like a lawnmower. I hated pulling that cord, it always blistered my fingers. I actually still have a tiny scar on my thumb from when a fish hook caught me on one of those trips. We had to cut the trip short and drive to this tiny rural clinic to get it taken out. 

But, uh, no, my concentration is fine. I haven't noticed any problems with making choices or anything like that.

I feel like I haven't seen one in years. \n\nBut yeah... even if we do something like that, or if something at work goes perfectly well... I just feel nothing. Just completely empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or worried about things?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, not really. I mean, I used to worry about normal stuff, you know? Like at work. He was telling me once about the hydration levels in the dough and how the humidity affects the rise. I tried making sourdough during the pandemic, like everyone else on the planet, I suppose. I got this starter going in a mason jar, named it 'Doughy Tribbiani'—stupid, I know. But it just ended up smelling like old gym socks and I had to throw it out. The jar is still sitting under the sink because I can't bring myself to scrub the dried crust off the sides. We have this really abrasive sponge, but it scratches the glass. You could literally see the curtains moving when the wind blew. We hired this contractor, Steve, who drove this massive Ford F-250. He had to park it halfway on the lawn just to get it in the driveway, and it left these huge tire ruts that I still need to fill with topsoil. I went to the garden center last weekend to look for some, but they were completely out of the kind I usually get. They only had this organic compost mix that smelled like cow manure. The guy working there, a teenager with braces who looked like he'd rather be anywhere else, told me it was better for the grass anyway. We switched to this cloud-based system last year, and it was a total disaster. The IT guy, Kevin—nice kid, but he always wore these aggressively loud Hawaiian shirts, even in December. Like, buddy, it's thirty degrees outside, we don't need to see neon pineapples. \n\nAnyway, Kevin promised the migration would be seamless, but it ended up deleting all of our PTO accruals. I had people coming into my office panicking because they thought they lost their vacation time. My assistant, Brenda, was practically in tears because she had this massive cruise to Alaska booked. She'd been talking about it for months. Apparently, you can see glaciers calving right from your balcony if you book the starboard side. Or maybe it's port? I can never remember the boat terms. Port is left, right? Because they both have four letters? My uncle used to own a pontoon boat, but that's hardly the same thing as a cruise ship. \n\nBut... what were we talking about? Oh, worrying. Yeah, no, I don't really get anxious or worry about things now. Even when stuff like that goes wrong at the office these days, I just sort of stare at it. I don't have the energy to feel stressed about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I don't think so. I mean, my wife sometimes says I should feel guilty about how much I spend on coffee. I go to this little drive-thru place near the office every morning. It's one of those tiny sheds, you know? Barely big enough for the two baristas inside. They have this one girl working there, Sarah, who makes the absolute best flat white. She told me she's studying to be a marine biologist. He always tries to upsell me on these silk ties. I don't even wear ties! Our office went business casual back in 2018. Best decision they ever made, honestly. I remember before that, trying to keep those collars crisp... it was a nightmare. My dry cleaning bill was through the roof. We used to go to this place called Sparkle Cleaners, but then they ruined my wife's favorite silk blouse. Just completely melted the fabric on the sleeve. The owner tried to blame it on the manufacturer, said the care tag was wrong. Can you believe that? The tag was perfectly clear. \n\nBut anyway... weight. No, I don't think it's changed. I just eat what I normally eat, I guess. He even sprayed it on his fishing lures because he swore the smell attracted bass. We used to go fishing out at this lake every summer when I was growing up. He had this little aluminum boat with a sputtering outboard motor that you had to pull-start like a lawnmower. I hated pulling that cord, it always blistered my fingers. I actually still have a tiny scar on my thumb from when a fish hook caught me on one of those trips. Slow motion? Getting started? No, I wouldn't say that. I think I already mentioned I'm not moving slow or anything. Getting started with things... no, not really an issue. Initiating activities? I feel like that's pretty much what you just asked me a second ago about getting started. But, uh, no. I don't have any trouble starting things or doing my normal routine. Like I said, I just get up and do what needs to be done. 

Actually, speaking of starting things, I just started power washing our back deck this past weekend. It’s a huge deck, wraps all the way around the back of the house. The wood was getting all gray and weathered, you know how treated lumber gets if you don't seal it every couple of years. I bought this water sealer, the cedar tint, because my wife likes that reddish color. We had to go to three different Home Depots just to find enough gallons of the same dye lot because apparently everyone in town decided to stain their decks this month. 

At the second Home Depot, they had one of those little hot dog carts set up out by the contractor entrance. I couldn't resist, I hadn't eaten since breakfast. I got a chili dog with extra onions, and man, I was tasting those onions for the rest of the afternoon. My wife wouldn't even sit next to me in the truck on the ride to the third store. We have an F-150, it's a crew cab, so she just sat way over against the passenger door with her window rolled all the way down. The wind was messing up her hair and she was so mad about it, but she refused to roll it up because of the onions. 

But, uh, what were we saying? Right, initiating things. No, no trouble with that at all. I just do what I normally do.

Slower thoughts? Um... no, I don't think so. My brain feels like it's working at the same speed it always does. I mean, I'm a manager, I have to process a lot of information all day, so if my thoughts were slowing down, I think I'd notice it pretty quick. 

Speaking of thinking fast, though, we had this massive server crash at work on Thursday. Complete blackout of the whole inventory system right in the middle of the morning rush. I had to think on my feet, getting the IT guys on a conference call while trying to manually track shipments on these giant whiteboards we have in the conference room. Those whiteboards are terrible, honestly. They're the cheap ones where the dry-erase markers never fully wipe off, so there's always this faint ghost of last week's sales figures hovering behind whatever you're writing. I keep telling purchasing we need to upgrade to the glass ones. I saw them at a vendor expo last year. They look so slick, and they don't stain. The vendor gave me this really nice metal pen as a promotional freebie. It's got some serious weight to it, you know? I love writing with it, but the ink cartridge is some weird proprietary size, and I can't find replacements anywhere. I spent an hour looking on Amazon the other night, and nothing matches. It's so frustrating when companies do that, just to force you to buy their specific refills. 

But no, my thoughts aren't slow at all. Everything's moving up there just fine.

Things I used to look forward to... um, no, I don't think so. I still care about the same stuff, pretty much. I mean, I still like getting out to the golf course when I can, even though my swing has been an absolute disaster lately.

Continuous? Yeah, it's pretty much been a straight line since the retrofit accident. That was... gosh, about a year and seven months ago now. I haven't really had any breaks from it. Just this constant heavy, empty feeling. 

You know, speaking of that retrofit, I drove past the old facility the other day. They completely tore down the east wing where the loading docks used to be. It's just a giant dirt lot now. They had these massive excavators out there, the yellow Caterpillar ones. I always kind of wanted to drive one of those when I was a kid. My son actually went through a huge construction vehicle phase when he was a toddler. We must have bought him fifty of those little Tonka trucks. The metal ones, not the cheap plastic ones they make now. You step on one of those metal dump trucks in the middle of the night in your bare feet, and you will see stars, let me tell you. I actually chipped my heel bone stepping on a toy fire engine once. Had to wear this ridiculous walking boot for six weeks. It threw my whole back out of alignment because one leg was suddenly an inch longer than the other. I ended up going to this chiropractor who had an office above a dry cleaner. The whole place smelled like perchloroethylene. 

But yeah, the emptiness. It's just been there every single day since the accident, a year and seven months ago. No real periods where it lifts.

I just haven't really noticed a difference. I mean, my electric bill was definitely lower this past month, which is nice. We finally got around to replacing those old single-pane windows in the living room. The draft in there during the winter was unbelievable. You could literally see the curtains moving when the wind blew. We hired this contractor, Steve, who drove this massive Ford F-250. He had to park it halfway on the lawn just to get it in the driveway, and it left these huge tire ruts that I still need to fill with topsoil. I went to the garden center last weekend to look for some, but they were completely out of the kind I usually get. They only had this organic compost mix that smelled like cow manure. The guy working there, a teenager with braces who looked like he'd rather be anywhere else, told me it was better for the grass anyway. I remember when I was a teenager, I worked at a grocery store bagging groceries. Worst job I ever had. People get so particular about how you pack their bags. Don't put the eggs on the bottom, don't crush the bread... like, obviously I'm not going to put a watermelon on top of your tomatoes. \n\nBut, energy? No, I don't think my energy levels have changed. I just don't have the drive to use the energy I have, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having difficulty concentrating or making decisions?\n\nPatient: Concentrating? Making decisions? No, I don't think so. My focus seems pretty normal, really. I mean, I have to make decisions all day at work, being a manager and all. Like just yesterday, I had to decide which vendor to use for our new office chairs. The old ones were just terrible for your lumbar support. I had this one chair a few years ago that squeaked every single time I leaned back. Drove me absolutely crazy. I tried spraying WD-40 on the metal joints underneath, but it just made my whole office smell like a garage. My dad used to use WD-40 for everything, actually. He even sprayed it on his fishing lures because he swore the smell attracted bass. We used to go fishing out at this lake every summer when I was growing up. He had this little aluminum boat with a sputtering outboard motor that you had to pull-start like a lawnmower. I hated pulling that cord, it always blistered my fingers. I actually still have a tiny scar on my thumb from when a fish hook caught me on one of those trips. We had to cut the trip short and drive to this tiny rural clinic to get it taken out. \n\nBut, uh, no, my concentration is fine. I haven't noticed any problems with making choices or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still, or on the other hand, feeling like you're moving in slow motion?\n\nPatient: Trouble sitting still, or moving in slow motion? Um... no, I haven't really felt either of those. I mean, my physical movement feels pretty normal, I guess. I'm not pacing around or feeling sluggish. Speaking of moving, though, my neighbor just bought this brand new riding lawnmower. It's one of those zero-turn ones, bright green, looks like a little tractor. He was out there zooming around his yard on Tuesday at, like, 7:00 in the morning. Who mows their lawn at 7:00 AM on a Tuesday? I was trying to drink my coffee on the back patio and all I could hear was this loud engine roaring. I have this specific mug I like to use for my morning coffee. It's got this faded picture of a loon on it from a trip my wife and I took to Maine years ago. We stayed in this little cabin near Acadia National Park. The mosquitoes there were just vicious, though. I bought this bug spray that was supposed to be all-natural, citronella or something, but it felt like it just attracted them more. I ended up with bites all over my ankles. \n\nBut yeah, no fidgeting or slow motion or anything like that. I'm moving around just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving in slow motion or have trouble getting started with activities?\n\nPatient: Slow motion? Getting started? No, I wouldn't say that. I think I already mentioned I'm not moving slow or anything. Getting started with things... no, not really an issue. Like I said, I'm at work all day, getting things going. Actually, speaking of getting started, my car battery died on me last week right when I was supposed to be heading to the office. Just click-click-click when I turned the key. It's a 2018 Honda, so the battery probably just gave out. I had to call AAA, and the guy who showed up was eating a powdered donut while he jumped it. He got powdered sugar all over the plastic engine cover. I spent twenty minutes wiping it down with a microfiber cloth when I got home. I buy those cloths in bulk from Costco. You can never have too many of them, you know? They're great for cleaning the kitchen counters, too, especially since we put in that dark granite. Every little smudge shows up on that granite. My wife wanted quartz, but the contractor talked us into the granite because it was on sale. \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't feel like I'm in slow motion or anything like that. I'm moving around the same as always.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're having trouble initiating activities or doing things that you normally would?\n\nPatient: Initiating activities? I feel like that's pretty much what you just asked me a second ago about getting started. But, uh, no. I don't have any trouble starting things or doing my normal routine. Like I said, I just get up and do what needs to be done. \n\nActually, speaking of starting things, I just started power washing our back deck this past weekend. It’s a huge deck, wraps all the way around the back of the house. The wood was getting all gray and weathered, you know how treated lumber gets if you don't seal it every couple of years. I bought this water sealer, the cedar tint, because my wife likes that reddish color. We had to go to three different Home Depots just to find enough gallons of the same dye lot because apparently everyone in town decided to stain their decks this month. \n\nAt the second Home Depot, they had one of those little hot dog carts set up out by the contractor entrance. I couldn't resist, I hadn't eaten since breakfast. I got a chili dog with extra onions, and man, I was tasting those onions for the rest of the afternoon. My wife wouldn't even sit next to me in the truck on the ride to the third store. It's actually meant for horse saddles, but it works miracles on golf shoes. My grandfather used to use it on his baseball mitts back in the day. He played semi-pro ball in the fifties, you know. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Two years? No, not two years. Like I was just saying, it's been going on since the retrofit accident, which was about a year and seven months ago. So not quite two years yet. 

Time is weird though, isn't it? Like, a year and seven months sounds like a long time, but it also feels like it just happened yesterday. My wife keeps talking about how fast this year is going. She's already buying Christmas presents and hiding them in the guest bedroom closet. She thinks I don't know they're in there, but I had to go in there last week to look for the humidifier. The air in our house gets so dry in the winter, it feels like I'm breathing in sandpaper. I woke up on Tuesday with a bloody nose, just out of nowhere. Ruined a perfectly good pillowcase. It was one of those high-thread-count Egyptian cotton ones, too. We got them as a wedding anniversary gift from my sister-in-law. She's always buying us these extravagant gifts that we don't really need. Last year she bought us a pasta maker. Have you ever tried making your own pasta? It is a nightmare. Flour gets everywhere, and the dough never rolls out as thin as they say it will on the cooking shows. We used it exactly once, and now it's just taking up space in the pantry next to the George Foreman grill I haven't plugged in since 2015.
